This included up-regulation of a putative Aplysia homolog of the C. elegans gene zig-4 (change in LFC = 0.50, padjusted = .002; XM_013081649), which is involved in axon guidance (e.g. Aurelio et al. 2002), HINT1 (change in LFC = 0.30, padjusted = .049; XM_005093232), a highly-conserved enzyme that mediates some forms of synaptic plasticity (e.g. Liu et al. 2017), and a number of transcripts potentially related to protein synthesis.

We also quantified global patterns of similarity in gene expression 1 day after standard and enhanced training. A signed-rank comparison showed a similarity in the ordering of gene expression lists that would rarely occur by chance (similarity score = 4492, p < .0001; Figure 3C). This is notably stronger similarity than what we have found 1 and 5 days after standard training (similarity score = 1,488, p < .0001; Rosiles et al., 2026). Finally, we also calculated the correlation in regulation across all transcripts flagged as clearly-regulated 1 day after standard or enhanced training (1,170 distinct transcripts; Figure 3D). We found a strong correlation (r = .71 95% CI [.68, .74]); rcorrected = .79; B = 0.39 95% CI [0.37, 0.42]). Notably, the slope of this relationship indicates an overall pattern of weakening regulation despite continued training, with the typical level of regulation around 40% of what is observed after the first round of shock. Again, this is a substantial attenuation in transcriptional response, but less than what would have occurred in the absence of additional training (r = .38 95% CI [.33, .43]; rcorrected = .54; B = 0.07 95% CI [0.06, 0.08]; Rosiles et al., 2026).

Standard training produces transcriptional changes that decay rapidly (Rosiles et al., 2026; Perez et al., 2018). Our results here show that enhanced training does not fully prevent this decay, but selectively extends regulation of a subset of transcripts, maintaining more of the initial transcriptional state than would occur in the absence of further training. In addition, enhanced training may activate a small set of distinctive transcriptional changes. Either of these differences could be relevant to the notable differences in forgetting induced by standard and enhanced training.

The transcriptional response to enhanced training decays rapidly but not completely How does the initial transcriptional response to enhanced training evolve over the course of sustained memory maintenance? To find out, we analyzed microarray data from pleural ganglia of animals harvested 5 and 11 days after enhanced training (N = 8 paired microarray samples).

Surprisingly, our array-wide screen identified no clearly-regulated transcripts 5 days after training and no clearly-regulated transcripts 11 days after training (Figure 4A). This was not exclusively due to low power, as both time points included 8 microarrays, and the estimated false-negative rate at each time point was 0.00. This does not preclude, however, some false negatives, especially given the stringent criteria we use for flagging training-regulated transcripts (see below).

To directly compare regulation across time points, we calculated changes in training effects from day 1 to the days 5 and 11 (Figure 4A). Of the 114 transcripts clearly regulated 1 day after training, 70 (61%) showed a clear and statistically significant difference in regulation at day 5 (marked in blue in Figure 4A). This was always in the direction of weaker regulation by day 5. At day 11, 93 transcripts (82%) showed a clear difference in regulation compared to day 1, and this was always for decreased regulation over time. Thus, we found a widespread and significant attenuation of gene regulation despite the continued strong maintenance of sensitization memory.

Assessing global patterns of gene expression showed that some aspects of regulation apparent 1 day after enhanced training are preserved at days 5 and 11. Analysis of gene ranking indicated similarity between day 1 and day 5 unlikely to occur by chance (similarity score = 5,214, p < .001; Figure 4B left).

Similarity between days 1 and 11 was weaker and restricted primarily to down-regulated transcripts, but still statistically significant (similarity score = 1,680, p < .001; Figure 4B right). We also assessed linear correlations in gene expression between time points across the 114 transcripts flagged as clearlyregulated 1 day after enhanced training. We found a continued strong correlation between days 1 and 5 (r = .74 95% CI [.65, .82]); rcorrected = .88; B = 0.45 95% CI [0.37, 0.52], Figure 4C left), but the slope indicates that typical levels of regulation at day 5 are only about 45% as strong as at day 1. Continued decay of transcriptional signal was evident by day 11 (r = .45 95% CI [.32, .61]); rcorrected = .34; B = 0.28 95% CI [0.18, 0.37], Figure 4C right). This is a notable decline in degree of correlation from day 1-5 to day 1-11 (rday1_day11 -rday1_day5 = -0.29, t = 6.26, df = 112, p < .0001), with only 20% of the variance in training-induced expression at day 11 related to what was observed at day 1. Still, these results indicate that the transcriptional signal induced by enhanced training has not fully decayed by day 11.

Because the global assessments suggest some continuation of regulation over time, we reassessed the microarray data from 5 and 11 days after training. Specifically, we conducted a focused analysis, using the same stringency but testing only the 114 transcripts that were flagged as regulated 1 day after training. This increases power by decreasing the number of comparisons and thus the aggressiveness of corrections for multiple comparisons. Note, however, that this analysis was exploratory and not part of our pre-registered plan. The focused analysis identified 13 potentiallyregulated transcripts 5 days after training and 5 potentially-regulated transcripts 11 days after training (3 of which were also on the 5-day list). These potential long-regulated transcripts are listed in Table 2 and marked in red in Figure 4A. All of these transcripts were regulated in the same direction as observed on day 1. Although this analysis is exploratory, the consistency of direction and the high overlap across time points demonstrate encouraging convergence. Moreover, 3 of the transcripts identified in this focused analyses were among the 7 we have previously identified as being verypersistently regulated after standard sensitization training (Patel et al. 2018; Perez et al. 2018), including a putative homolog of an ionotropic glutamate receptor, GRIK1 (Day 1: LFC = 0.93; padjusted = 0.004; Day 5: LFC = 0.51; padjusted = 0.01; Day 11: LFC = 0.36; padjusted = .30; XM_035969615).

Overall, these results suggest that the transcriptional changes evident 1 day after enhanced training attenuate dramatically and rapidly, despite the continued strong expression of sensitization memory. A handful of transcripts, though, may show continued changes in expression, including some which are also persistently activated by standard training.

Attenuated transcription after enhanced sensitization is not due to bilateral changes in gene expression

We wondered if the apparent decline in regulation 5 and 11 days after regulation could be due to an increase in regulation on the untrained side of the nervous system rather than a decline in regulation on the trained side. Specifically, we noted that enhanced sensitization training sometimes produced increased T-SWR responses on the untrained side of the body. Although we excluded these animals, we worried that the enhanced protocol might produce some latent sensitization encoded in weak or late-breaking transcriptional changes in the untrained pleural ganglia. To explore this issue, we conducted a single-channel analysis of our microarray data, comparing control measures taken at days 1, 5, and 11. This analysis was not part of our pre-registered plan. As the majority of training effects at day 1 were up-regulation, we looked specifically for transcripts showing higher control expression at days 5 and 11, as this would specifically obscure any continued training effects.

Across the array, we found 4 transcripts which showed a clear increase in expression on the untrained side from day 1 to 5 and an additional 207 transcripts from day 1 to 11. None of these potential increases in control expression, however, were in transcripts which had shown a clear decay in training effect over time. Although this does not rule out bilateral training effects, it shows that these could not account for the sharp decrease in transcriptional regulation we observe after enhanced sensitization training.

qPCR shows good convergent validity with microarray results and suggests additional long-regulated transcripts

We used qPCR to validate some of the key microarray findings, testing the same samples plus 3 additional 1-day and 3 additional 5-day samples which had not fit on the arrays. First, we selected a transcript (XM_005099124) which shows very strong regulation 1 day after standard training (LFC = 1.18, padjusted < 0.000001) and which was flagged for a statistically-significant decline in regulation 1 day after enhanced training. With qPCR we indeed found much weaker regulation 1 day after enhanced training declining even further to negligible levels at days 5 and 11 (Figure 5A). Next, we tested two transcripts (ApEgr and APBiP) which microarray indicated show a strong decay from days 1 to 5 after enhanced training; qPCR showed similar results (Figure 5, panels B and C).

Finally, because our exploratory focused analysis suggested that both standard and enhanced training might share a set of very-persistently-regulated transcripts, we tested 3 transcripts which we have repeatedly found to be regulated long after sensitization training: ApFMRFa (NM_001204546.1) and two uncharacterized transcripts (FF066943 and EB350631). As shown in Figure 5 (panels D, E, and F) all 3 of these transcripts showed significant enduring regulation long after enhanced sensitization training. This suggests that both standard and enhanced training produce similar long-lasting transcriptional changes, despite their very different trajectories of memory expression.

Overall, pPCR results closely tracked microarray results (compare open squares and filled triangles in Figure 5), and there was a strong correlation in mean log-fold changes across these techniques (r = 0.73 across the 6 transcripts at 3 time points, p = 0.0006).

Discussion

Here we re-confirmed a notable training effect for long-term sensitization of the T-SWR in Aplysia: while a standard training protocol (1 day of painful shocks) produces a long-term sensitization memory that is completely forgotten over the course of a week, enhanced training (4 consecutive days of painful shocks) produces a sensitization memory still very strongly expressed 11 days after training.

In some systems it can be difficult to dissociate the effects of additional training on encoding and forgetting. Consistent with prior studies (Frost et al. 1985; Wainwright et al. 2002), our behavioral results show that enhanced sensitization training influences both of these memory processes, producing stronger initial encoding (larger % change in T-SWR 1 day after training) as well as slower forgetting (smaller % relapse towards baseline responding).

Our microarray results provide a start towards trying to understand how additional training might modulate the encoding and forgetting of long-term sensitization. Interpretation is clouded by several limitations in the current study: 1) our comparisons between standard and enhanced training are across independent studies rather than conducted together in the same study, 2) our microarray is based on an early EST library which does not represent all neuronally-expressed genes and where annotations are often lacking, and 3) we conducted the analysis on whole ganglia containing a variety of cell types, and 4) our 1-day results likely miss some regulated transcripts due to loss of 1 array at this timepoint. We are working on a follow-up study to directly compare standard and enhanced training protocols in samples of homogenous cell types with an updated microarray design. In the meantime, our data provides some suggestive clues about how extended training might modulate sensitization memory.

One intriguing finding is that additional training seems to become less effective at producing transcriptional changes in the pleural ganglia. One day after standard training we observe strong regulation of over 1,000 transcripts (Conte et al. 2017; Rosiles et al. 2020). Here we found that most of these transcriptional changes are not sustained by additional training: only 6% of transcripts regulated after standard training still show clear regulation after enhanced training, 22% show significantly weaker training effects, and the average training effect is only 40% as strong as after standard training. Thus, there seems to be a trend towards de-coupling the experience of painful stimuli from transcriptional changes. For example: a single round of shock produces very strong and consistent increases in ApCREB1 expression for at least 1 day after training (Conte et al. 2017), but we found only negligible changes in ApCREB1 a day after a 4 th round of shock. Thus, the repetition of noxious stimulation seems to de-couple pain signaling in the T-SWR circuit from the further activation of ApCREB1 as well as many of the other transcripts regulated in naïve animals by the same stimuli. We can only speculate about how such a de-coupling might occur, but one possibility would be depletion/desensitization in the serotonergic neuromodulatory system that helps encode initial sensitization (reviewed in Calin-Jageman et al. 2024b). Consistent with this possibility, we have found that standard training decreases the expression of a putative serotonin receptor as well as three of the four Aplysia homologs of adenylyl cyclase (Conte et al. 2017). On the other hand, sensitization training increases nociceptor excitability and synaptic strength (Scholz and Byrne 1987; Cleary et al. 1998), changes that might otherwise be expected to enhance the ability of additional training to sustain gene expression changes.

Although some transcripts seem to become less responsive to additional rounds of training, others show continued or even new sensitivity to shock. Specifically, we observed that enhanced training sustains the regulation of 68 transcripts activated by standard training while also producing notably stronger activation of 20 transcripts. Among the sustained changes (Supplemental Table 1), there was a clear motif of up-regulation of transcripts related to transcription and translation, potentially representing ongoing demands for protein production to encode the enduring form of sensitization memory. Among the possible enhanced-specific changes (Table 1) are additional transcripts potentially related to protein production, a putative homolog of a key synaptic plasticity enzyme (HINT1), and a putative homolog of a transcript that plays a critical role in axon guidance and maintenance (the C elegans gene zig-4). The latter is intriguing given that outgrowth of VC axons is observed after enhanced but not standard sensitization training (Wainwright et al. 2002). More research will be needed to investigate if up-regulation of this putative zig-4 homolog may be a transcriptional marker for neuron growth that helps distinguishes the encoding of forgettable and enduring forms of sensitization memory.

Another notable aspect of our findings is that the transcriptional changes induced by enhanced sensitization training fade rapidly but not completely. The vast majority of transcriptional changes detectable 1 day after enhanced training show clear and statistically-significant declines in regulation, with mean training effects declining to 45% as strong by day 5 and 28% as strong by day 11. Because our analysis was based on whole ganglia, we cannot distinguish to what degree this attenuation represents a) declining regulation within each cell type initially regulated by training, b) a decline in the number of transcriptionally-responsive cells, and/or c) late-developing offsetting transcriptional changes. Regardless of mechanism, this clear attenuation of transcriptional regulation is not complete: we detected a somewhat preserved overall transcriptional signal, and both focused tests and qPCR reveal that a few transcripts show detectable levels of regulation long after enhanced training. This includes multiple transcripts likely to influence ongoing neuronal signaling, including up-regulation of a putative glutamate receptor (XM_035969615) and up-regulation of the mRNA encoding the peptide neurotransmitter Phe-Met-Arg-Phe-amide (FMRFa; M11282; NM_001204546). These long-regulated transcripts substantially overlap with those we have found after standard training despite the very marked difference in trajectory of memory expression. Indeed, the finding of continued up-regulation of FMRFa challenges our proposal that up-regulation of this transmitter after standard training contributes to forgetting (Calin-Jageman et al. 2024a), as here we observe a similar long-lasting upregulation but with minimal forgetting.

The sustained regulation of gene expression after learning we observed here is consistent with proposals that transcriptional positive-feedback loops play a role in memory maintenance (e.g. Smolen et al. 2019; Liu et al. 2011). However, the long-regulated transcripts we were able to detect do not include ApCREB1 nor any other annotated transcription factor that might suggest how a sustained transcriptional response is organized. Moreover, the function of a sustained transcriptional response remains unclear, as we detect many of the same changes during retention after enhanced training (this paper), during forgetting after standard training (Patel et al. 2018; Perez et al. 2018), and 1 day after a brief re-training re-expresses a forgotten sensitization memory after standard training (Rosiles et al. 2020). One possibility is that this small set of persistent transcriptional changes represents a sustained memory trace, but that the accessibility/expression of this trace is modulated based on training type, time, and arousal state. It is also possible, though, that memory maintenance does not involve the metabolic cost of ongoing transcription, and that our observations of persistent transcriptional changes after sensitization training are merely artifacts of the training protocol or cellular stress. In addition to a cell-specific survey of transcriptional changes induced by sensitization training, functional studies will be needed to better characterize how learning-induced transcription relates to the encoding, maintenance, and forgetting of sensitization memory.

Materials and Methods

We pre-registered our sample size plan, data exclusions, all manipulation and measures, and a draft microarray analysis script. We report all pre-registered analyses and note exploratory analyses developed in consultation with the data. Our preregistration documents are posted to the Open Science Framework (https://osf.io/wvx6z/); the microarray data is also posted to NCBI's Gene Expression Omnibus (Geo ID: GSE350967); our finalized analysis scripts and behavioral data are posted to Zenodo (Calin-Jageman and Calin-Jageman 2026).

Animals

Animals (100-125g) were obtained from the RSMAS National Resource for Aplysia (Miami, FL) and maintained at 16 o C in one of two 90-gallon aquariums with continuously circulating artificial sea water (Instant Ocean, Aquarium Systems Inc.). Handling was as described previously (Herdegen et al. 2014b). Aplysia are true hermaphrodites.

Enhanced sensitization training

A four-day long-term sensitization training protocol (Figure 2B) was used, adapted from Wainwright et al. (2002) but with a stronger shock (90mA vs. 60mA) and a constant-current stimulus (Bonnick et al. 2012; Cyriac et al. 2013). Animals were trained on 4 consecutive days. Each day of training consisted of 4 rounds of noxious shock applied at 30 minute intervals to one side of the body with a hand-held electrode. Each round of shock consisted of 10 pulses (60Hz biphasic) of 500ms duration at a rate of 1hz and an amplitude of 90mA. During the course of each shock, the stimulating electrode was slowly moved from anterior (just behind the neck) to posterior (just in front of the tail) and back to cover nearly the entire surface of that side of the body. Side of training was counterbalanced.

Behavioral measurement

As a behavioral outcome, we measured the duration of the tail-elicited siphon-withdrawal reflex (T-SWR; see Walters and Erickson 1986). The reflex was evoked by applying a weak shock to one side of the tail using a hand-held stimulator (60Hz biphasic DC pulse for 500ms at 2ma of constant current). T-SWR behavior was measured as the duration of withdrawal from the moment of stimulation to the first sign of siphon relaxation. Behavioral measurements were made by a researcher blind to the side of training.

To track sensitization memory, we measured T-SWR durations before training (Baseline) and then 1, 5, and 11 days after long-term sensitization training (Post-Tests). At each stage, 5 T-SWR measurements were made on each side, with a 10 minute interval between each behavioral measurement We focused on changes in T-SWR behavior by calculating the log-fold change from Baseline to Post-Test at each timepoint on both the trained and untrained side of each animal:

By this metric a score of 0 represents no change in T-SWR duration, and scores above 0 represent sensitization. We calculated the degree of sensitization expression at each time point by comparing the change in T-SWR behavior on the trained side to the untrained side: Sensitization_Expression = LFCTrained -LFCUntrained Isolation and processing of pleural ganglia RNA We compared gene expression in the pleural ganglia on the trained vs. untrained sides. Pleural ganglia RNA was isolated immediately after the 1, 5, and 11-day post-tests These ganglia contain the VC nociceptors which help mediate the expression of long-term sensitization memory through traininginduced long-lasting increases in excitability and synaptic efficacy (Cleary et al. 1998). Isolation and homogenization was exactly as described in Herdegen et al. (2014b). To control for lateralized gene expression, tissue from two animals trained on opposite sides were pooled.

Comparison to Transcriptional Correlates of Standard Training

Our microarray analysis compared gene expression 1, 5, and 11 days after enhanced long-term sensitization training (4 daily training sessions). To help provide context for these comparisons we also re-analyzed gene expression from a previous study examining transcriptional changes 1 day after "standard" long-term sensitization training (1 training session; Conte et al., 2017; GEO GSE95596). This archival data captures the transcriptional changes that occur after a single set of painful shocks, providing context for how 3 additional rounds of training impact gene expression.

Sample size determination

We set a target of 8 microarray samples per time point (requiring 16 animals per time point).

Our previous work has shown good sensitivity with 8 microarray samples. Our sample size target exceeds the consensus recommendation of at least 5 biological replicates per group (Allison et al. 2006; Pavlidis et al. 2003; Tsai et al. 2003).

Quality controls

We ran animals in batches of 12-30. We completed training of 113 animals, but excluded animals based on pre-registered quality controls:  Animals had to exhibit strong learning, defined as at least a 30% increase in T-SWR duration from baseline to the 1-day post-test. Only 1 animal failed this quality control.

 The expression of sensitization had to be unilateral, with less than a 30% increase in T-SWR on the untrained side from baseline to the 1-day post-test. A total of 20 animals failed this quality control, though almost always with a weaker change on the trained side than the untrained side. None of these seemed clearly related to a protocol error; instead it seems that our training protocol had a weak tendency to induce bilateral sensitization.

 There could be no protocol errors in training or dissection. A total of 1 animal was excluded based on this criteria due to the erroneous application of a shock on the untrained side of the body.

 The RNA isolation step had to produce sufficient yields for microarray analysis with no DNA contamination and relatively even concentrations across the trained and untrained samples. A total of 10 samples (20 animals) had to be discarded due to poor RNA isolation.

In addition, we lost a batch of 8 animals due to a tank failure and 4 animals were not dissected because within their batch the application of quality controls left no opposite-side-trained animal to pool with. This left 60 animals with valid behavioral data yielding 30 total samples for transcriptional analysis: 11 harvested 1 day after training, 11 harvested 5 days after training, and 8 harvested 11 days after training.

We selected 8 samples at each time point for microarray analysis; the additional samples were held for qPCR validation. One of the 1-day microarray samples had a processing error leading to ambiguity about sample labelling; it was discarded from all microarray analyses.

Microarray processing

We used the Aplysia Tellabs Array (ATA: GEO: GPL18666) to characterize changes in gene expression due to long-term sensitization training. This array includes 26,149 distinct probes representing all known sources of Aplysia californica ESTs and mRNAs at the time of design (January 2012). Based on estimates from previous microarray designs (Moroz et al. 2006), the ATA should cover >50-60% of all transcripts expressed in the nervous system. Full details on the array design are reported in Herdegen et al. (2014b).

Microarray processing was completed by ArrayStar Inc. (Rockville, MD). A two-color approach was used with each array hybridized to a sample from a trained or untrained pair of animals. In half of cases, trained samples were hybridized with Cy3 and controls to Cy5; the other half were dye-swapped.

Processing was exactly as described in Herdegen et al. (2014b).

We matched the microarray probes to the current annotation of the Aplysia genome available in NCBI. For those probes with a definitive match, we identify them by the accession number of their matching gene model, otherwise we use the EST or mRNA accession number used in the original microarray design.

Statistical analysis

Behavioral responses were averaged by time point. Paired comparisons were made from baseline to post-test for each side using the esci package for R (Calin-Jageman and Cumming 2024; Cumming and Calin-Jageman 2024). Standardized effect size estimates (Cohen's d) are corrected for bias (Hedges 1981) and calculated so that positive values represent an increase in response (sensitization).

Microarray data was analyzed using limma (Smyth 2005; Ritchie et al. 2015) from the Bioconductor suite of tools (Gentleman et al. 2004) for R (Ihaka and Gentleman 1996). Our processing script for identifying differentially regulated transcripts was pre-registered and is posted on the Open Science Framework. Median expression values were analyzed (Zahurak et al. 2007). These were corrected for background using the normexp+offset algorithm recommended for Agilent microarrays (Ritchie et al. 2007). An offset of 30 was selected based on inspection of MA Plots. Expression was then normalized using the loess function (Smyth and Speed 2003). Where multiple probes were used to measure the same EST or mRNA, these were averaged.

Identification of regulated transcripts

At each time point, trained and control expression were compared using an empirical Bayesmoderated t-test (Smyth 2004). Statistical significance was calculated using Benjamini-Hochberg correction for multiple comparisons to maintain a 5% overall false-discovery rate (Benjamini and Hochberg 1995), and we report FDR-adjusted p values as pcorrected. We used the treat function from limma (McCarthy and Smyth 2009) to conduct a stringent test for significant regulation. Specifically, rather than use a null hypothesis of no regulation, we tested for regulation statistically distinguishable from at least a 10% change in expression in either direction. We term transcripts flagged with this stringent criterion as "clearly regulated" and we have shown that transcripts identified at this stringency show very strong predictive validity in independent qPCR (Herdegen et al. 2014b; Holmes et al. 2014).

We examined the completeness of each gene list using the propTrueNull function (Ritchie et al. 2015) and the convex decreasing densities approach developed by Langaas et al. (2005). We then calculated the false negative rate as 1 -%regulated -%truenull, and the expected number of false negatives as the false negative rate times the number of non-significant transcripts.

We also conducted exploratory analyses testing contrasts between different time points (e.g. comparing treatment effects 1 and 5 days after training), equivalent to testing for a training x time interaction. These tests were again conducted against an interval null of +/-10% with a adjustment to maintain an overall 5% FDR.

Similarity of ranked transcript lists

We used the OrderedList package for R to calculate similarity or regulation between time points (Lottaz et al. 2006). For each time point, transcripts were first ranked by strength of evidence (corrected p value) and sign of regulation (up-regulated or down-regulated). We then computed and assessed the degree of similarity in ranked lists across the top 1000 transcripts in each direction.

Correlation of training effects

We also estimated correlations in training effects across time points. For each comparison, we restricted down to the union of transcripts flagged as regulated at two time points. We then calculated the correlation in log-fold change scores across these transcripts. We calculated each relationship using a simple Pearson's r and with correction for possible measurement error using the genuine association of gene-expression profiles function (geneas) in limma (Ritchie et al. 2015).
